expresses the required constraint reliably.\nThe language research brief selects any missing native capability for this project.\nUse parsing or dependency analysis for semantic code rules rather than fragile text matching.\nPropose a custom checker only when existing tools cannot express the constraint reliably.\nSeparate formatting from design checks so cosmetic changes cannot conceal an architectural failure.\nAvoid arbitrary file, function or rule-count limits without a project-specific reason.\n\n## Example mappings\n\n| Principle | Concrete rule | Enforcement | Required evidence |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Keep persistence behind one interface | UI modules cannot import the database adapter | Import boundary analysis resolving project paths and aliases | An allowed public interface import passes and a direct database import fails |\n| Write each rule once | Generated settings match the authoritative source | Deterministic regeneration and comparison | A stale generated value fails |\n| Validate external input | Reject invalid input at the actual public boundary | Behaviour tests and applicable schema or type checks | Invalid input is rejected and valid input succeeds |\n| Keep behaviour stable during cleanup | Existing supported journeys remain compatible | Relevant integration or end-to-end checks | The same supported journeys pass before and after the change |\n| Tests protect agreed behaviour | Tests call the public interface and take expected results from an approved source | Human review against the acceptance source | A test asserting private implementation, or an expectation copied from implementation output, is identified in review |\n| Keep dependencies purposeful | New abstractions serve a current use and a clear responsibility | Human design review against vision and technical design | Review identifies the requirement, responsibility and simpler alternative |\n\nDo not disguise a human design judgement as a deterministic violation.\nGive the reviewer the relevant authority, example and evidence instead.\n\n## Proof required before adopting an automated rule\n\nRequire a representative valid case and a deliberate violation in disposable fixtures.\nThe valid case must pass.\nThe violation must fail with the expected diagnostic and exit code.\nThe clean project must pass the selected final checks.\nUse the selected tool's native rule-testing mechanism where available.\nInclude relevant syntax, aliases, re-exports, generated code and documented exceptions in the proof scope.\nRecord unsupported syntax and dynamic dependencies as coverage limits rather than assuming they pass.\n\nAfter preparing native dependencies in the clean and independently seeded fixtures, run `rule-proof --repo <target> --check-plan <file> --check-id <id> --valid <clean-dir> --violation <seeded-dir> --expect <specific-diagnostic> --json`.\nThe plan follows `schemas/check-plan.json`; the selected check supplies the native child-command object.\nThe command runs separate disposable copies, records versions, exits and diagnostics in scratch, and removes the copies.\nRepeat for each private import, alias or equivalent resolution bypass and forbidden cycle, retaining public imports and permitted dependency direction in the clean control.\nUnavailable native tools, interrupted execution and failed clean setup are blocked, never proof.\n\nCapture the maintained command, tool version, output, exit code and relevant input state directly from execution.\nThe same maintained command must work locally and in the project's CI when the project selects one, without ignoring failures.\nFor behaviour compatibility, preserve agreed acceptance outcomes rather than proving only that unit checks are green.\nThese are evidence requirements for selected enforcement, not a development workflow.\n\n## Integrate the maintained command\n\nUse reviewed selected findings and `apply` to extend the project's existing check command rather than creating a competing entry point.\nThe target repo owns each checker or pins its declared development dependency; running checks must not require an installed bstack skill or agent session.\nScope each package rule in its native configuration or checker, and prove a sibling package's permitted use remains valid.\nApply the complete initial documented baseline through selected debt findings before expecting the audited project to pass.\nKeep every known violation visible with its reason and removal condition; new violations fail and fixed entries must be removed.\nContinue to prove each rule independently on clean fixtures without counting unrelated audited debt as new violations.\n\nFor integration proof, select the maintained command in the check plan, for example executable `npm`, args `[\"run\", \"check\"]`, cwd `.` and versionArgs `[\"--version\"]`.\nRun the same rule-proof interface against separate disposable sources containing the integrated command, installed native dependencies and the accepted baseline, with one new seeded violation in the negative source.\nCapture both exits and the specific new diagnostic; a tool setup failure does not prove enforcement.\nThe valid control must include a permitted sibling-package use where package scope matters.\n\nIf the project selects CI, apply the same maintained command to its configuration as another reviewed edit.\nAn edit that integrates selected checks declares optional `checkIntegration`, a nonempty list of unique command paths, each a nonempty array of string keys.\nFor `package.json`, mark each selected script with a path such as `[[\"scripts\", \"check\"]]`; other changed scripts receive no integration validation.\nFor a selected `.yml` or `.yaml` CI edit, use JSON syntax, which is valid YAML, and mark the exact `run` or `script` paths, such as `[[\"jobs\", \"check\", \"steps\", \"0\", \"run\"]]`.\nThe installed runtime supports only this JSON subset for marked CI edits; it uses Node built-ins and does not parse general YAML.\nEvery selected path must resolve to a string command in the complete proposed bytes; missing commands, deletion and unsupported marked formats fail before writes.\n`continue-on-error` and `allow_failure` on the selected CI command's ancestor objects must be literal `false` when present.\nUnmarked edits, other package scripts, other CI commands and unrelated configuration fields pass through the ordinary protected-write validation unchanged.\nThe plan digest binds these selections alongside the complete proposed bytes.\nSelected commands use literal arguments and fail-fast top-level `&&` chains; line breaks between commands are allowed only after `&&`.\nTokens are nonempty unquoted words or whole double-quoted literals, separated by whitespace.\nOnly ASCII spaces and tabs separate tokens; LF line breaks are allowed after `&&`, and leading/trailing spaces, tabs and LF are ignored.\nCarriage returns are unsupported anywhere in a command, including CRLF, quoted tokens and leading/trailing whitespace.\nUnicode whitespace remains literal; other ASCII control characters are unsupported.\nUnquoted `<` and `>` redirection, including attached forms, and unquoted leading executable `@` prefixes are unsupported in every command of a chain.\nDouble-quoted redirection characters and `@` remain literal, as do unquoted `@` argument characters.\nSingle quotes anywhere, mixed quoted/unquoted tokens and adjacent quoted fragments are unsupported in executable and argument positions.\nBackslashes and shell escaping are unsupported, including inside quoted arguments; use forward-slash paths and quotes for literal spaces.\nPercent and exclamation characters are unsupported in every script token, including double-quoted literals, because Windows can expand environment variables despite quotes.\nDollar signs and backticks are unsupported in every token, including double-quoted literals.\nUnquoted wildcard and bracket patterns, tilde expansion, variable and command substitution, brace expansion, extglobs and caret escaping are unsupported in executable and argument positions.\nQuoted wildcard, bracket and tilde characters remain literal, as do direct child-command argument arrays, which run without a shell.\nEvery decoded word is rejected if it contains `|`, `&`, `;` or a newline, including quoted arguments.\nQuotes do not make shell control syntax acceptable as an argument.\nFailure-masking operators (`||`, pipelines, separators, background execution and negation), unquoted shell comments, assignment prefixes, shell dispatch and substitutions fail with `ignored-check-failure` before any writes.\nShell dispatch includes `command`, `builtin`, shell executables, executable paths to those programs and their `.exe`/`.cmd`/`.bat` forms in every `&&` segment.\n`npm exec`/`npm x` and `npx` reject `-c` and `--call` modes, including attached option values; `env`, including `env -S`, remains unsupported.\nThis bounded grammar is a configuration safeguard, not an interpreter for arbitrary shell programs or a proof of a checker's implementation.\nThe identical maintained command must still pass the valid disposable control and reject the seeded violation with its diagnostic and nonzero exit through `rule-proof`.\nCI examples are unverified adapters until actual hosted execution is observed; local disposable proof does not establish a merge gate.\n\n## Control existing debt\n\nFix selected findings and keep the remaining findings visible.\nWhen immediate cleanup is unsuitable, propose a narrow record of known violations for the user to accept as temporary debt.\nPrefer the native tool's baseline or suppression mechanism where it exists.\nNew violations must fail, and fixed entries must leave the debt record so it shrinks as fixes land.\nDo not silently refresh a baseline to accept new failures.\nAn exception must record its reason, scope and removal condition.\nNever disable a whole rule or exclude a whole directory merely to obtain a green result.\nA changed-file-only check cannot establish a constraint that depends on the complete import graph or generated outputs.\n\nWhere native research found no baseline feature, use `schemas/baseline.schema.json` and `baseline check --repo <target> --baseline <repo-relative-file> --violations <file> --json`.\nSupply the complete current native violations as rule/path/key objects, preserving stable identities when lines move.\nKeep native execution failures separate from the violation list; unavailable analysis cannot establish an empty list.\nChecking reports retained debt and rejects new violations or entries whose violations were fixed.\n`--refresh` removes fixed entries but cannot accept new debt by itself.\nAdding one current violation requires `--refresh --finding <id> --findings <file> --entry <file>` for a selected unresolved debt finding covering that path, with a narrow reason and removal condition.\nA failed refresh changes no baseline bytes.\n\n## One authoritative source per rule\n\nPreserve an existing standards source that already serves reviewers.\nMissing `CODING_STANDARDS.md` is not a readiness failure.\nCreate that document only when non-mechanical review rules need a distinct maintained home.\nAn existing design section can suffice for a small project.\nKeep review standards actionable and scoped, with a reason, example and exception policy where needed.\n\nTool configurations own formatting values and executable constraints.\nStandards link to those checks rather than copying settings.\nAgent instructions point to the project's rules, evidence requirements and maintained check commands while preserving distinct scopes.\nIf a selected edit moves a review rule, replace its old text with a link to the new authority.\nDesign documents may explain the structural decision without maintaining a second copy of the standard.\nRead relevant existing decisions before proposing a conflicting rule.\nKeep proposals as drafts until selected and preserve review before apply for documentation as well as code.\n# Maintenance contract\n\nRecord change evidence against the project's maintained contract.\nRoutine maintenance uses the approved project sources and the current diff; it does not repeat the goal interview or the full repo audit.\nResolve the target, authorised change, explicit comparison base, contract path and selected review or delivery process first.\nRead only affected sources and implementation, widening that scope when the evidence exposes a dependency or unresolved decision.\n\n## Contents\n\n- Collect the final scope\n- Assess every candidate\n- Validate locally\n- Validate a standalone checkout\n- Supply the selected delivery gate\n\n## Collect the final scope\n\nUse Node 24 or later and Git.\nRun these commands from the installed skill, passing the target explicitly.\n\n```sh\nnode scripts/repo-audit.mjs contract validate --repo <target> --contract .bstack/project.json --json\nnode scripts/repo-audit.mjs evidence collect --repo <target> --base <ancestor-commit> --json\n```\n\nCollect early to identify affected sources, then recollect after implementation so the assessment covers the final committed, staged, unstaged and new paths, including both sides of renames and deleted paths.\nUse `--base empty` only before the first commit.\nAn unavailable base blocks comparison; fetch the required object rather than substituting a passing empty diff.\nFor a shallow checkout, run `git fetch --unshallow origin`, then `git fetch origin <required-base-commit>`.\nThe default contract is `.bstack/project.json`; pass `--contract <repo-relative-path>` when the project selected another location.\nCustom previous authority needs `--previous-contract <base-policy-path>` on collection and validation.\nKeep previous and proposed coverage when scopes, checks or document registrations change.\nA missing or incompatible previous policy requires reconciliation or a reviewed migration, not silently reduced coverage.\nA first contract requires the explicitly selected foundation finding.\n\nCollection returns a scratch assessment skeleton at `data.path` without editing the project.\nComplete that change-specific record instead of adding an agent activity log to the design or glossary.\nRecompute candidates after source changes, integration or rebasing, and refresh review and affected execution evidence when relevant inputs or the comparison base change.\n\n## Assess every candidate\n\nInspect each candidate document against the intended behaviour and the actual before/after implementation.\nAssess every unmapped path explicitly too; unknown paths cannot bypass impact review.\nUse the installed `schemas/evidence.schema.json` and `schemas/impact-assessment.json` formats.\nEach entry names `changedBehavior`, all relevant `changedPaths`, a concrete `reason`, literal source `citations` with `path`, `pointer` and `version: base|current`, and `dependentWork`.\nA document entry must cite that authoritative document itself.\nList affected authoritative rule IDs in `coverage`.\n\n| Change | Source to inspect | Assessment |\n|---|---|---|\n| Concept, name or meaning | Relevant glossary | Update confirmed language or explain the preserved definition |\n| Responsibility, interface or dependency | Design and accepted decisions | Describe the actual structure and any approved decision change |\n| Review expectation or repeated error | Existing standards source | Clarify the rule or propose a deterministic check |\n| Purpose, boundary or non-goal | Vision | Surface the intent decision before dependent implementation |\n| Setup, commands or document location | Contributor guidance and agent pointers | Keep maintained command and authority links accurate |\n| Internal change preserving a contract | Relevant sources for that area | Explain specifically why the documented behaviour remains true |\n\nChoose `updated`, `no-impact` or `decision-needed` for each entry.\nAn update includes `delta: {\"before\": \"old excerpt\", \"after\": \"new excerpt\"}` naming the actual changed definition or rule; empty excerpts represent additions or deletions.\nA changed date, comment, whitespace or hash alone is not substantive maintenance.\nKeep correct documents unchanged.\n\nA substantive no-impact reason connects the concrete implementation change to the cited invariant that still holds.\nFor example: “The pricing implementation now names its intermediate total; DESIGN.md still requires quantity multiplied by the unit price, and the public quote and checkout outcomes are unchanged.”\nSupport that claim with the actual diff, literal authoritative excerpts and relevant executed checks.\n“Internal change”, “tests pass” or “docs unaffected” alone does not explain impact.\nAn existing failing journey remains failed even if the changed function's unit checks pass.\nIf code violates an approved rule, propose correcting the code rather than changing the rule to excuse it.\n\nUse `decision-needed` with concrete `dependentWork` when intent, accepted architecture, an exception or conflicting authority needs an owner decision beyond the authorised scope.\nKeep dependent work blocked until the decision is resolved and the assessment is reviewed again.\nReplacing an acceptance source requires the old case, approved new case, affected work and a separate approval citation in `decisions`.\nPassing checks cannot resolve a pending decision or authenticate approval.\n\n## Validate locally\n\n```sh\nnode scripts/repo-audit.mjs docs check --repo <target> --json\nnode scripts/repo-audit.mjs docs generate --repo <target> --check --json\nnode scripts/repo-audit.mjs evidence validate --repo <target> --base <ancestor-commit> --assessment <assessment.json> --json\n```\n\nUse the same selected `--contract` throughout.\nFor stale generated facts, run `docs generate` without `--check` to render proposals in scratch, review the exact delta, then apply selected edits through the protected apply path.\nValidation recomputes coverage and returns `data.fingerprint`, including while review or required execution is missing.\nHave the selected reviewer assess the substantive claims against the live diff before recording that value in the assessment's top-level `fingerprint`.\nBinding a fingerprint is not automatic semantic approval.\nRun required previous and proposed leaf checks through `run-checks` using the reviewed `schemas/check-plan.json` format, then attach `execution: [{\"runId\": \"<run-id>\", \"plan\": \"<plan-file>\"}]` and validate again.\nPlans include the exact declared commands and version probes, check input scopes, changed and deleted inputs, both policy locations and authoritative sources.\nKeep capture references outside leaf product scopes.\nRefactors require passing original-state protection before edits and compatibility checks afterwards; bug fixes require a captured failing reproduction before the fix and a passing reproduction afterwards.\nOutside dependencies also require the matching live `probe record` before/after pair and successful `probe compare`.\nUnavailable, stale, skipped or failed required evidence cannot pass.\nLocal scratch captures cover declared inputs and are not portable attestations.\n\n## Validate a standalone checkout\n\nFor portable review, commit implementation first, then collect with `--portable .bstack/assessment.json` and the explicit base.\nCopy the returned skeleton to that selected path, complete and review it, obtain and bind the fingerprint through `evidence validate`, and commit the assessment separately.\nPortable records use `repo: \".\"` and retain the reviewed implementation commit in `head`; only the selected assessment may change afterwards.\nPass one explicit record rather than guessing the newest evidence.\n\n```sh\nnode .bstack/bin/bstack-check.mjs --repo <target> --base <ancestor-commit> --assessment <target>/.bstack/assessment.json --json\n```\n\nThe installed dependency-free checker needs Node and Git, with no skill or agent session.\nRun it in the working checkout and a different-root clean clone with isolated home and cache, the same explicit base and the unchanged committed assessment.\nIt validates structure, executes each distinct required leaf once with fresh results, then validates the results and review inputs again without editing the assessment.\nEarlier saved successes cannot replace that invocation's checks.\nThe selected base must equal the computed merge base of the explicit source and base objects.\nChanges to the checker or contract also execute the base's checker from scratch against the proposed tree; missing or incompatible prior checkers block for migration and explicit prior coverage.\nFor independent delivery checks, extract and invoke the prior checker with those same explicit inputs too.\nRecollect, review, bind and commit again after rebase or base changes.\n\n## Supply the selected delivery gate\n\nReturn the assessment, exact source and comparison objects, validation verdicts, fresh `checker-result.json` path, command captures, acceptance coverage, unresolved decisions and coverage limits to the project's selected reviewer or delivery gate.\nKeep logs local unless that delivery process requires them; use committed portable review when it needs clean-checkout evidence.\nThe delivery process must actually invoke the maintained aggregate against submitted changes and assess the semantic claims.\nIt may be local review, a chosen pipeline or selected CI; do not start another delivery service or assume hosted CI.\nAdding instructions, a hook or a workflow does not establish enforced merge protection.\nReport failed and unverified outcomes honestly: scripts establish coverage and execution within the supported contract, while reviewers judge meaning and authorisation.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

Whenever you are about to give user a complex response that will be easier to understand via a rich / interactive page, consider using Lavish Editor. First generate an interactive HTML artifact according to user request, then run `lavish-axi <html-file>` so the user can visually review it, annotate elements or selected text, queue prompts, and send feedback back through `lavish-axi poll`.\"\nvisual_guidance[5]: \"Use visual hierarchy to make the most important decisions, risks, tradeoffs, and next actions obvious at a glance\",\"Show, don't tell: explain concepts, flows, relationships, and comparisons with labeled illustrations - hand-authored inline SVG, per the diagram playbook - and show existing UI or state with screenshots of the real pages (run the app read-only if needed); If the user explicitly ended the session from the browser, this refuses to reopen it and explains why instead of reopening uninvited - pass `--reopen` only when the user asks for further review or something important needs their visual attention\",\"Unless the user specifies another location, create HTML artifacts in the current working directory under `.lavish/`\",\"Lavish serves the html file through a local express.js server. If your html needs to reference other filesystem assets such as images, CSS, fonts, and local scripts, copy them into the same directory as the HTML file, then reference them with relative paths from that directory. Never prepend `/` to those asset paths - root paths won't work\",\"Run `lavish-axi poll <html-file>` to wait for user feedback. It long-polls and stays silent until the user sends feedback, ends the session, or leaves every review window disconnected past the reconnect grace period, so leave it running - never kill it. Detected layout issues never return this poll: the browser files them in the user's Layout issues inbox in the Lavish top bar, and they arrive as an ordinary tag \\\"layout-warnings\\\" prompt only when the user selects them and queues the fixes. Never edit the artifact to chase a layout issue the user has not queued. The only exception is a fatal artifact_failures response, which means the review surface itself could not be used. Keep the poll in the foreground by default and let it return the feedback directly to the agent. A background poll is allowed only through a harness-native tracked background-job facility whose completion result is guaranteed to resume or notify the same agent. Never use `nohup`, shell `&`, `disown`, redirected fire-and-forget processes, or a detached terminal without an explicit verified callback merely to keep polling alive. If the harness has no completion-aware background facility, use the foreground poll or first wire a verified wake callback into the surrounding supervisor. Do not tell the user the artifact is being monitored until that wake path is live. If the poll gets killed or times out before feedback arrives, re-run it - feedback remains queued until delivery. Poll delivery consumes the response, so read it completely. If it returns browser_disconnected, the review window stayed disconnected past its reconnect grace period but the session remains resumable; ask the user whether to reopen or end it, and do neither uninvited. Codex detected: completed background tasks may not resume Codex automatically, so keep the poll attached to the active turn. `Send & End` ends the session. Its final feedback is still delivered once. After that response, polling stops, and the agent must not reopen the session uninvited.\",\"Mermaid is the whiteboard opt-in, not the diagram default: only when the user asks for an editable whiteboard, author that diagram as Mermaid in a `.mermaid` container. Rendered Mermaid diagrams there become embedded, editable Excalidraw whiteboards in the browser (click a diagram to unlock editing; a Fullscreen action opens it over the whole viewport) - flowchart, sequence, class, ER, and state diagrams convert to editable shapes; other types embed as an image to draw on. Scenes autosave locally; an unmodified autosave silently re-converts when a reload changes the Mermaid source. If the reviewer edited the scene, they choose to re-convert and discard saved edits or keep editing the saved scene. Standalone and exported copies still render plain Mermaid. Queue feedback adds a prompt to the Conversation panel; when the user sends it, poll returns a tag \\\"whiteboard\\\" prompt carrying a bounded edit summary plus local scenePath (.excalidraw JSON) and previewPath (PNG) files - read the summary first, open the files only when needed, then apply the edits by updating the Mermaid source in the artifact (never try to write the scene back)\",\"Run `lavish-axi end <html-file>` to end a session as the agent - ending it this way still allows a plain reopen later. When the user ends it from the browser instead, a later `lavish-axi <html-file>` refuses to reopen it without `--reopen`\",\"Run `lavish-axi export <html-file> [--out <path>]` to write a portable copy of the artifact - one HTML file with its LOCAL assets inlined - so it opens with no Lavish server and no sibling files. Remote CDN/font references are left as links, so it needs network to render those. Users can also export from the browser chrome's overflow menu\",\"Run `lavish-axi share <html-file> [--private | --password <pw>]` to publish the artifact on ht-ml.app (https://ht-ml.app), a third-party hosting service not part of Lavish, and get back a visitable URL. Shares are PUBLIC by default, so anyone with the link can open them. Pass --private to publish a PRIVATE page behind a password Lavish generates and returns - hand the user that password with the URL and say it is a shared secret anyone they give it to can use; --password <pw> uses one you were already given instead. Local assets are inlined; remote refs load over the network. It also returns a secret update_key that can republish or lock the same URL later - run `lavish-axi share --help` before using it. Users can also publish from the browser chrome's overflow menu\",Run `lavish-axi stop` to shut down the background server (it also self-stops when idle or after the last session ends with nothing connected),\"Run `lavish-axi playbook <playbook_id>` for focused artifact guidance. One artifact often combines several playbooks (for example a plan that includes a comparison and a diagram), so MUST open each matching playbook before writing HTML.\",\"Lavish does not auto-inject any design system - artifacts stay portable so they render identically when opened directly without lavish-axi running. Before writing any HTML: Decide the design direction in this strict priority order, and only move to the next step when the current one truly yields nothing: (1) if the user asked for a specific look or named design system, use that; (2) otherwise you must first inspect the project the artifact is about - the subject or product whose content or UI it represents, which may differ from your current working directory - and match that project's design system: Tailwind or theme config, shared CSS variables or design tokens, component library, brand assets, or existing styled pages. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_19","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc 'cat [isolated-home]/.agents/skills/repo-audit/references/architecture.md'","aggregated_output":"# Architecture\n\nUse this reference when recommending module boundaries.\nStart with the reported area, stated next change, approved design and relevant history.\nExpand only where evidence leads.\nFor a new project, review expected changes against proposed boundaries instead of inventing history.\n\nCoupling means that a change needs knowledge of, or edits to, another part.\nCohesion means that code belongs together for a clear responsibility.\nA module is a logical unit with an interface and implementation, which may span several private files.\nA god file mixes unrelated responsibilities and becomes a shared target for different changes.\nLength alone does not establish that problem.\n\n## Principles to consider\n\nChoose principles only where project evidence supports them.\nNo single architecture style is required.\n\n| Principle | Practical rule | Source |\n|---|---|---|\n| Single responsibility and cohesion | Keep code together when it changes for the same business reason and separate unrelated reasons | [Single responsibility](https://blog.cleancoder.com/uncle-bob/2014/05/08/SingleReponsibilityPrinciple.html) |\n| Information hiding | Hide a changeable design decision behind an interface so callers do not depend on its representation | [Module decomposition](https://www.cs.lafayette.edu/~gexia/cs301/resources/parnas.html) |\n| Locality of change | Keep capability code and tests together where this reduces coordination across unrelated areas | [Vertical slices](https://www.jimmybogard.com/vertical-slice-architecture/) |\n| Explicit dependency direction | Define allowed dependencies and check forbidden imports and cycles across intended boundaries | [Dependency hierarchy](https://www.cs.lafayette.edu/~gexia/cs301/resources/parnas.html) |\n| Separate wiring from behaviour | Startup assembles modules while modules own business behaviour | [Composition roots](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2011/07/28/CompositionRoot/) |\n| Proportional deployment choices | A single deployed application can retain firm module boundaries | [Module boundaries](https://www.martinfowler.com/articles/microservice-trade-offs.html) |\n\nA deep module hides useful complexity behind a small interface.\nConsolidate tightly related shallow modules when removing their layer concentrates complexity in one place.\nSeparate unrelated responsibilities when their changes currently require shared edits.\nAvoid forwarding layers that add navigation without hiding useful complexity.\nOrganise ownership around domain knowledge rather than scattering one rule across load, validate and save phases.\nThese adaptations follow the pinned design and module guidance attributed in the package NOTICE.\n\n## What the audit inspects\n\n- Unrelated business responsibilities, or startup, UI, domain rules and persistence combined without clear internal boundaries.\n- Imports of private implementation, leaked representations and cycles across intended module boundaries.\n- Shared mutable state and multiple writers of canonical data.\n- Frequently changed files and pairs that repeatedly change together.\n- Central registries, broad utilities, shared type collections and wiring files that unrelated work repeatedly edits.\n- Tests requiring unrelated modules or asserting implementation details rather than a public contract.\n- Two representative planned changes, their write paths and the shared contracts they would modify.\n\nCollect dependency edges through the native analysis selected by the language research brief.\nResolve aliases, re-exports and package entry points.\nReport unsupported syntax, dynamic dependencies and unresolved imports as coverage limits.\nUnresolved imports do not demonstrate independence.\nPrefer an existing architecture checker to another dependency engine.\n\nFor history, record revision range, rename handling and exclusions for generated files, lockfiles and broad formatting changes.\nShow the supporting commits behind a finding.\nCo-change is distinct from observed merge conflicts.\nHistory may omit abandoned rebases and conflict resolutions.\n\n## Signals that are not violations\n\nSize, import counts and change frequency indicate where to investigate.\nThey do not prove a bad boundary.\nA large cohesive domain owner may be sound.\nA thin startup file may legitimately import many modules for wiring.\nA stable domain contract may have many callers.\nCo-change may reflect good cohesion, a mechanical commit or harmful coupling.\nInspect the reasons before recommending a split or consolidation.\nDo not infer conflicts or prescribe services from these signals.\n\n## Boundary rules\n\nName each module's responsibility, owned knowledge, public contract and private implementation.\nDefine allowed dependency direction and the scope of forbidden imports and cycles.\nCallers use the public interface and do not depend on private storage or representation.\nPrivate implementation may span files without exposing additional public entry points.\nKeep startup focused on assembly and put behaviour with its domain owner.\nRemove unnecessary shared write targets before serializing work.\nWhere sharing is real, enforce one writer and sequence unavoidable shared edits.\n\nReview two representative changes against proposed boundaries.\nFor example, a pricing-policy change and a notification-template change should remain inside their owners when the public contracts still suffice.\nCheck whether either also changes a shared type, registry or data representation.\nCompare declared paths and contract changes, then review semantic dependencies even when paths are disjoint.\nA script reports overlap evidence but cannot prove independence or predict every conflict.\nFewer unrelated shared edits are an expected benefit, not a measured reduction until actual evidence exists.\n\nRecord each proposed boundary's project-specific reason, source, scope, enforcement and exception policy.\nReuse existing design decisions and standards rather than duplicating their rules.\nPresent evidenced candidates and alternatives for selection before applying changes.\nVerification requires representative allowed and forbidden imports and compatible public behaviour, not file-count targets.\nUse rule-proof for a clean public-import control and independent private, alias or equivalent resolution bypass and cycle violations with the selected native check.\nThe Python equivalent-resolution fixture exercises an intermediate public-looking bridge to private implementation; proof does not establish every possible re-export or dynamic dependency.\nMaintain these coverage limits alongside the rule evidence.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

conventions:\n\n```md\n# Ordering\n\nThe context that receives and tracks customer orders.\n\n## Language\n\n**Order**:\nA customer's request for a set of products.\n_Avoid_: Purchase, transaction\n\n**Customer**:\nA person or organisation that places orders.\n_Avoid_: Client, buyer\n```\n\nA domain context is an area with its own meanings and responsibilities, such as ordering or
